# Chapter Two.

141. And I can never thank my **God** enough that He has led me, the most unworthy of all, into the **poverty of Christ** <note>This refers to the spiritual discipline of "Gelassenheit" or yieldedness, where one abandons worldly wealth and ego to mirror Christ’s humility.</note> itself, and has unlocked its mysteries through <term>practice: "Praxi," the lived experience and application of spiritual truths rather than mere academic study</term>; although it is despised by all people, which happens due to a lack of righteous, fiery love for **Jesus Christ**.

142. To Him, as our Head, King, and Prince of the Throne—indeed, our eternal Bridegroom—we should rightly present ourselves in total conformity and be obedient, loving Him above all things. For where there is no conformity <note>original: "Gleichförmigkeit"</note>, there can be no unity or harmony.

143. Now it is known to us, as much from Holy Scripture as from the <term>light of nature: "Licht der Natur," a term used by mystics like Böhme and Paracelsus to describe an innate, God-given understanding of the natural and spiritual worlds</term>, that **Adam** turned his free will away from his Creator and toward the spirit of this world. He desired this world’s treasures and splendor, thereby bringing all of us, his children, into such false addiction and desire, making us slaves of the world and children of darkness.

144. Thus we now eat our bread in the sweat of our brow and spend our lives in nothing but weariness, anxiety, distress, grief, and sorrow; and finally, we must leave everything behind again and pass out of this world into eternal darkness, remaining children of hell.

145. Unless it happens that we turn back and put on **Jesus**, who came forth from His Heavenly Father into our humanity to help us once more. He came to lead our adopted, creaturely will away from all earthly addiction and desire, back to His and our Heavenly Father...

## Chapter Two.

141. And I can never thank my God enough that He has led me, the most unworthy, into the **poverty of Christ** <term>poverty of Christ: "Armuth Christi," a spiritual and often physical state of self-denial and simplicity intended to mirror Jesus’s humble life</term> itself, and has unlocked its hidden mystery through **practice** <term>practice: "Praxi," referring to lived experience rather than mere theoretical study</term>; even though it is despised by all people, which occurs because of a lack of righteous, fiery love for Christ Jesus.

142. To Him, as our Head, King, and Prince upon the Throne, indeed our eternal **Bridegroom**, we should rightfully present ourselves as conformed in all things and be obedient, also loving Him above all else: For where there is no conformity, there can also be no unity or harmony.

143. Now, it is known to us as much from **Holy Scripture** as from the **Light of Nature** <term>Light of Nature: "Licht der Natur," the innate human capacity for reason and moral understanding provided by God</term>, that **Adam** turned his free will away from his Creator and toward the spirit of this world, desiring the treasures and splendor of this world. In doing so, he brought all of us, his children, into such false craving and desire, making us slaves of the world and children of darkness.

144. Thus, we now eat our bread in the sweat of our brow and spend our lives in nothing but misery, anxiety, need, grief, and worry; and finally, we must leave everything behind once more and depart from this world into eternal darkness, remaining children of hell.

145. ## The Second Chapter.

...then everyone wanted to stumble and rub against this stone; everyone said, "Who can live like that?"

152. And they condemned the **poverty of Christ** and those who love it as the very worst deceivers, the likes of which have not been since the world began. They claimed such people would be highly harmful and intolerable to all well-ordered **republics** <term>Republiquen; here referring generally to organized states or commonwealths</term>; the bell was already cast, but the clapper was missing. <note>A German idiom suggesting that the plans for their persecution were ready, but the final spark or excuse to act had not yet arrived.</note>

153. This storm tested us not a little and drove us to prayer, because the devil fully intended to make an end of us.

154. Reason may now twist the Scriptures and create a foreign understanding however it wants, yet Christ’s life and teaching stand directly opposed to the **Adamite natural life** <term>Adamischen Naturleben; the "fallen" state of humanity driven by selfish desires rather than God's will</term>; and it is impossible for someone to be a true **reborn Christian** who possesses anything in his will.

155. For in possession lies **self-will** <note>original: "Eigenheit," referring to the ego's attachment to "me and mine"</note>, which Adam put on: This must now be entirely denied, or it will be thrown into hell, where it belongs. Since Christ Himself lived in the world on alms and did not have so much property that He could lay His head upon it.

156. Now, Reason will suggest: "If all people were such faithful poor, who then should sustain them?" Listen, Reason: Christ’s teaching does not take away the **unrighteous Mammon** <note>A biblical term for wealth or ill-gotten gains</note> and does not abolish its right use: For it is a gift of God, with which the rich should trade and increase.

157. Christ calls them **stewards** and **bailiffs** <term>Rentmeister; financial administrators or treasurers who manage property for a master</term>, to whom He [entrusts] his faithful poor followers...

# Of the Natural Man.

...commanded, that they should give them their **necessities** <term>Nothdurft; the basic requirements for survival, such as food and clothing</term> at the right time; they desire nothing more.

158. And whoever says that his goods are his own, and belong to him, he is a **thief of God**, from whom he steals them, and he will have to give an account for it. God created everything to be held in common for the **common good**, not for gluttony, splendor, and lust.

159. And those rich people—who have locked God’s wealth in their chests and made it their own property, but have not tossed the poor, believing Christians their necessities at the right time—will be forced to hear with terror:

> and all devils! 
<note>This is a reference to the Gospel of Matthew 25:41, a cornerstone text for radical pietist views on social justice and charity.</note>

160. 161. The **Poverty of Christ** is the fortress of heavenly wisdom, wherein she dwells protected: Whoever wishes to enter into her must first become poor. The Poverty of Christ is the hidden treasure of all riches, powers, knowledge, and the **mysteries of God**. A fool despises it; but a wise man holds it in high value. All earthly **Mammon** <note>A biblical term for wealth or profit, often personified as a false god.</note> is to be counted as mere dung in comparison.

162. I speak in truth without vain glory, and the Lord is my witness: If someone were to offer me the goods of the whole world in exchange for the Poverty of Christ,

# The Second Chapter.

then everyone wished to stumble and rub themselves against this stone; each one said, "Who can live in such a way?"

154. **Reason** <term>Vernunft; human logic or worldly wisdom used to justify self-interest over divine command</term> may now twist the **Scripture** and create foreign meanings as it pleases, yet the life and teaching of Christ stand in direct opposition to the **Adamic natural life** <term>Adamischen Naturleben; the fallen state of humanity characterized by selfishness and pride</term>; and it is impossible for one to be a true **born-again Christian** who possesses anything within his own will.

155. 157. Christ calls them **stewards** and **estate managers**, to whom He [entrusts] His poor, believing followers

...for everyone wanted to stumble and rub themselves against this stone; everyone said, "Who can live like this?"

152. And they condemned the **Poverty of Christ** and those who love it as the very worst deceivers, the likes of which have not existed since the beginning of the world. They claimed such people were highly harmful and intolerable to all well-ordered **republics** <term>Republiquen; here referring to any organized state or commonwealth</term>. The bell was already cast, but the clapper was missing. <note>A historical idiom meaning that the plan for their persecution was fully prepared, lacking only the final spark or excuse to be set in motion.</note>

153. This storm tested us not a little and drove us to prayer, because the devil fully intended to make an utter end of us.

154. Reason may now twist the Scriptures and create strange interpretations as it wishes, yet Christ’s life and teaching stand in direct opposition to the **Adamic natural life** <term>Adamischen Naturleben; the fallen human state driven by self-preservation and worldly desires</term>. It is impossible for anyone to be a true, reborn Christian while possessing anything within their own will.

155. For within possession lies that **self-centeredness** <term>Eigenheit; the spiritual state of claiming "ownership" over one's life or goods, which the author views as the root of the Fall</term> which Adam put on. This must now be completely denied, or it will be cast into hell, where it belongs. Since Christ Himself lived in the world on alms and had not even so much property as a place where He could lay His head.

156. Now Reason will object: "If all people were such faithful poor, who would then support them?" Listen, Reason: Christ’s teaching does not take away the **unrighteous Mammon** <note>original: "ungerechten Mammon" — a reference to Luke 16:9, meaning worldly wealth</note> and does not abolish its proper use. For it is a gift of God, with which the rich should "increase their yield." <note>original: "wuchern" — in this context, it refers to the Parable of the Talents, meaning to use wealth fruitfully for God's purposes rather than for selfish gain.</note>

### Preface.

->I.<-

In this adjacent figure, which is the second by our author, it appears in what manner **faith** has now come through the forms of outer nature (among which the first three forms of the **Center of Nature** <note>original: "Centri Naturæ". In the theology of Jacob Boehme, these represent the harsh, contracted qualities of existence before they are transformed by light</note> are eternally understood as the dark world) all the way to the center point in the heart.

2. There, Jesus rises in the heart, breaks the dark womb <note>The "dark womb" refers to the unregenerate state of the human soul</note> and is born in us, takes up His kingdom in our heart, and as the **Serpent-treader** <term>Schlangentreter: A title for Christ based on Genesis 3:15, referring to the one who crushes the head of the serpent</term> promised by God—whose rising and future is from now on without end in us—He here, with the holy **birth of light** <note>original: "Lichtsgeburt". A mystical term for the spiritual illumination or "new birth" of the soul</note>, treads upon the head of Satan, the old serpent, without ceasing,

treads, and continually crushes the poisonous serpent-beast and casts it into the abyss.

Luke 15:22.

4. 5. This is also a long-lasting struggle, where love and wrath sharpen one another, and in which wrath must ultimately reveal love; it proceeds through all ten forms <note>While many mystical systems of this era use seven "qualities" or "forms," this author refers to a ten-part progression toward spiritual perfection</note> of the inner life of fire, up to omnipotence; there it becomes manifest that love alone is **God** and almighty, and that wrath is only nature, which must surrender its strength and power to love, in such a way that from both, one essence and being is formed.

6. In this state, the entire Godhead rises in the spirit, and God the Father and Son embrace one another in the newborn humanity, where the Father hands over humanity to the Son and presses it to His heart, betrothing it to **Jesus** in the Virgin of His Wisdom <note>Often referred to as "Sophia" in mystical literature, representing divine wisdom</note>; from then on, Satan always falls like lightning from heaven to the earth and is cast out, and we are overabundantly victorious in every struggle.

## Of the Reborn Person.

### I.

The kind reader should know that I do not write from opinions or books, but rather from my own vision and experience. I find great difficulty in this because this "new person" is spiritual and very hidden; he cannot be depicted with natural figures <note>original: "natürlichen Figuren"; meaning physical shapes or worldly metaphors</note>, nor can he be exalted by an earthly tongue.

2. For this reason, I must make use of natural parables. Therefore, the loving reader will have to contemplate these until he has attained the proper understanding and concept.

3. And if, through this, he should become eager to know and see himself in the light of divine wisdom, he must turn into his innermost <term>ground or center of the soul: The "Seelengrund," a mystical concept referring to the deepest part of the human spirit where God dwells</term> with truly fiery earnestness. He must pray diligently, calling upon God for His Holy Spirit, and surrender himself entirely—in body, soul, and spirit—with all that he is, has, and can do. He must vow loyalty and make a solemn resolution never to turn away from Him, whether in poverty, through the cross <note>referring to personal suffering or trials</note>, in need, or in death; but rather to follow steadfastly until his end, just as I have also done.

4. Thus, I do not doubt in the least that God will hear his prayer in the name of **Jesus**, and give what his heart has desired and will continue to crave.

5. The new body is as vastly different from the old as the bright-shining sun is from the dark earth; and although it already stands within the old body—

<summary>The author explains that the spiritual "reborn person" is a hidden, experiential reality that defies simple description. He invites the reader to seek this transformation through intense prayer, total surrender to God, and inward contemplation of the soul's center.</summary>
<keywords>rebirth, soul-ground, divine wisdom, earnestness, prayer, Jesus, spiritual body, experience vs. book-learning</keywords>

14. This heavenly essence of the new spiritual body draws the converted soul back into its fiery essences through its strong desire—as a bridegroom draws his dear bride—and brings forth a highly triumphant, bright, white light in the innermost part of the mind and soul, within the world of light.

This refers to the difficult spiritual process of bringing forth the "new man" or Christ within the soul.</note> they barely let a crumb fall under their table, which will likewise be a shame for them before God.

In 17th-century mystical German, this does not mean modern empirical science, but an internal, certain knowledge or "knowing-awareness" granted by divine illumination.</note> opened in him by God, as the Lord himself reveals this. Luke 12:47; James 4:17. <note>These verses state that those who know the right thing to do but do not do it are guilty of greater sin.</note>

21. And I hope that such kindhearted brotherly admonition shall not be interpreted poorly: for we must let our light shine, without respect of person, and commit the judgment to God.

22. Certainly, God holds dearly a free will that is surrendered to Him at its very ground <note>original: "Ihme zu Grund gelassenen Willen." This refers to "Gelassenheit," the state of being yielded or surrendered to God's will.</note>, wherein He Himself may will, build, form, and create what is pleasing to Him, and He reveals Himself to it in a friendly manner; but He hates a self-will, even if it performs many good works; for it works only in <term>Selfhood</term>.

-># Of the Reborn Person.<-

worthy teacher and leader within himself, and to such a person we have also not written.

24. To the inexperienced and unpracticed mind, however, we should let our light shine, and show what **rebirth** is; what process **God** maintains with the <term>old Adam</term> <note>original: "alten Adam." A theological term for the fallen, sinful nature of humanity inherited from the first man.</note>, and how the new man must proceed from one degree to another, until his new body has reached its full growth.

25. And it must be well noted that in rebirth we receive no new soul; but indeed a new body; and thus the soul has no need of rebirth; but only a renewal and turning from the external into the internal, where it is <term>renovated</term> <note>original: "renoviret." In this context, it implies a restoration to an original, pure state.</note> with the pure **Godhead**.

26. The old <term>maggot-sack</term> <note>original: "Madensack." A vivid, derogatory term used by 17th-century mystics like Jacob Böhme to describe the physical, mortal body as a vessel for decay.</note> is and remains fragile, and with its anxious hunger and driving, it contributes as much to the matter as manure on a field does to the growth of the grain.

27. It devours all the soul's provisions, brings it into poverty, anxiety, worry, and grief, so that the soul can no longer find any food, any lust, any joy nor delight in the external <term>swinish life</term> <note>original: "Säuleben." Refers to a life led by animal instincts and base desires.</note>; it becomes sad, contemplates the toil of earthly life, sees the *vanity* in all things, and begins to think of its Father’s house.

28. And if this anxious driver were not present, **God's** wonders of wisdom would not be brought to light, nor could the prayer of the believers be earnestly and fiercely kindled.

### The Third Chapter.

17. ...you might leave to your children. If you sought a pious soul for them, it would be much better.

18. Indeed, even the pious wealthy are blind and blameworthy in this regard, though they possess very good knowledge: in that they help their carnal worldly friends along with hundreds <note>Likely referring to hundreds of florins or units of currency.</note>, thereby strengthening them in their malice and idleness, and knowingly fattening the devil of <term>self-centeredness</term> <note>original: "Eigenheit." In 17th-century German mysticism, this refers to the "own-self"—the ego or fallen state of being centered on oneself rather than God.</note> within them.

19. ### Chapter Three.

29. To what end Christ leads his followers immediately into his holy poverty, robs them of all earthly means, banishes and persecutes them, and leaves them not even enough means upon which they can lay their head; so that they must place their trust in God and believe his promises, that the Father cares for them. <note>This is an allusion to Matthew 8:20, where Jesus says the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head.</note>

30. For poverty and need teach one to call out and cry, and to ask God for the strengthening of faith, which the students of Christ understand best.

31. And although this may find no credit among the wealthy, it is nevertheless the truth, which would testify for itself at once if they were to be robbed of all their provisions for just one day, as unbelief, doubt, care, fear, and sorrow would immediately set in within the mind, saying: "Where shall bread come from in the desert?" as we ourselves see in the case of Moses. Numbers 11, 13. <note>In this biblical passage, the Israelites despair over their lack of resources in the wilderness, showing a lack of faith in God's providence.</note>

32. We poor humans do not know how deeply we are swallowed by the <term>Spirit of the World</term> <note>original: *Spiritu Mundi*. A term used in alchemy and Christian mysticism to describe the vital, yet temporary and fallen, animating force of the material universe.</note> and live in pure unbelief; we often imagine a strong faith for ourselves, yet do not trust God for a piece of bread, fearing immediately—when we are taught to fear, love, and trust God above all things—that in doing so, we would be tempting God.

33. I have felt my weakness enough and experienced the <term>tremblings of my heart</term> <note>original: "Zaplen." Likely referring to *Zappeln* (struggling or twitching) or the "valves" of the heart, indicating the author's personal inner turmoil and lack of spiritual steadiness.</note>, until after much practice the small, weak branch of faith became a strong tree, which could stand against all the storm-winds of the Devil and of <term>Reason</term> <note>original: *Vernunft*. In this context, "Reason" refers to the human intellect that relies only on physical evidence and logic, which often opposes spiritual faith.</note>.

<meta>catchword: 34.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that Christ leads his followers into physical and spiritual poverty to force a total reliance on divine providence rather than earthly means. The author reflects on how human "Reason" and the "Spirit of the World" hinder true faith until one undergoes the trial of having nothing.</summary>

# Concerning the Reborn Person.

34. Now when the soul turns around within the body, it turns its back to the sunlight and turns its face toward God in the inner **World of Light**, and immediately receives a different sense of sight, hearing, smelling, tasting, and feeling, even though it still stands in the darkness. <note>The author describes a sensory "reorientation" where the spiritual senses are awakened even while the physical body remains in the material world.</note>

35. And it first recognizes that it has turned away from God and left its Father’s eternal house, and has also squandered its noble <term>life of love</term> <note>original: *Liebeleben*. In this context, it refers to the soul's innate divine vitality or the "inheritance" of grace.</note> with harlots; it falls upon its knees, humbles itself before its heavenly Father dwelling in the **hidden light**, and wishes to begin to pray in the spirit; yet it cannot, for it has been led to pray from books and does not yet understand true prayer in **spirit and truth**. <note>A reference to the Prodigal Son (Luke 15) and the idea that formal, "book-learned" prayer is insufficient for true spiritual rebirth.</note>

36. Then the **Holy Spirit** is sent forth into the heart with a very perceptible movement, as is indicated in this figure with the dove. <note>This likely refers to an illustration in the original manuscript or printed edition showing a dove descending upon a heart.</note> This movement is unaccustomed and strange to the soul, because it does not yet know God, and therefore wonders at it and searches into the movement.

37. Which the **Devil** perceives, slipping externally into the <term>mind</term> <note>original: *Gemüth*. A central term in German mysticism referring to the seat of emotions, will, and thought.</note> and seeking to make the soul doubtful with false suggestions, as if they came from him; as also happened to me, when the Devil spoke into my mind: "Now I have possessed you, you are mine!"

38. Which so terrified my soul that I rose from prayer and took hold of the **New Testament** to read therein, so as to drive away these evil thoughts.

### Chapter Three.

39. And when I opened it, these words of Paul in 1 Corinthians 6:19 caught my eye: "Do you not know that your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit, who is in you, whom you have from God, and that you are not your own?"

40. These words first brought me into the deepest amazement, for I had sought God outside of myself since my youth, walking sadly in the fields for many a half-day, or hiding myself in a <term>trench</term> <note>original: *Schanze*. Likely referring to a secluded field fortification or ditch where he could seek privacy for prayer.</note>, gazing at the sky and longing to speak with God, just as Moses, David, and other men of God I had read about in the Bible had done.

41. But to me, no God would appear, and I had to return home saddened: There I would then seize my prayer books, open the windows, or sit under the open sky so that my reading might rise unhindered toward heaven: And thus I went along blindly for 26 years. <note>The author highlights his former belief that God was physically distant (in the sky) and that physical obstacles or closed windows might block his prayers.</note>

42. For that reason, I immediately laid down my New Testament and fell back upon my knees to thank God for this <term>revelation of grace</term> <note>original: *Gnadenerscheinung*. This term describes a sudden, profound experience of divine favor or a "showing" of God's presence.</note>; which flowed forth so abundantly that I could reach no end for five hours, and even had to marvel at this rich blessing myself.

43. Immediately after, I also bound my heart with Christ and surrendered myself entirely to Him in body, soul, and spirit, being willing to carry whatever <term>cross</term> <note>A common metaphor for the hardships and social stigmas one must endure when following a radical spiritual path.</note> might be pleasing to Him, on the condition that He should not let me go alone,

...since I was an uncomprehending child, who could not distinguish between left and right.

44. Which my dearest <term>Helper</term> <note>original: *Gehülfe*. In the Pietist tradition, this likely refers to Christ as a personal companion and guide.</note> also kindly and lovingly accepted, and poured me a full cup, which was indeed sweet in the mouth, but uncommonly bitter in the belly. <note>This is a biblical allusion to Revelation 10:9, suggesting that while his spiritual awakening was joyous at first, it led to difficult consequences in the physical world.</note>

45. But He never left me alone, but rather faithfully rescued me in all temptations, sufferings, poverty, persecutions, and tribulations. To Him be eternal honor, praise, glory, and thanks!

46. I was also immediately enlightened with divine knowledge, and prompted in my mind to rebuke the evil life and to show myself to the priests, who immediately rang the alarm bell and handed me over to the authorities as a heretic, <term>enthusiast</term>, <term>visionary</term>, <term>Anabaptist</term>, etc. <note>original: *Enthusiasten, Fantasten, Wiedertäufer*. These were common pejorative labels used by the established church to criminalize radical reformers and those who claimed direct inspiration from God.</note>

47. Who subjected me to all sorts of mockery, shame, and disgrace, led me through the streets with bailiffs, and wanted my head off; but because they could not all agree on it, they finally confiscated everything and banished me from the city forever.

48. While I now lay locked in such a stinking hole, the Devil tried his first luck and tempted me with horrible despair, that I should seize the knife and release my anxious life from the torment, or make a short end with my neckerchief.

49. He also brought me into such a fiery wrestling and struggling that the flesh was worn away from my knees, leaving them bare; yet because of the internal soul-anguish, I felt no external pain; for the Devil made all prayers seem doubtful to me.

# The Third Chapter.

39. And when I opened it, these very words of Paul in 1 Corinthians 6:19 caught my eye: "Do you not know that your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit, who is in you, whom you have from God, and you are not your own?"

40. These words first brought me into the deepest wonder, for since my youth I had sought God outside of myself; I had wandered sadly in the fields for many a half-day, or hidden myself in a trench, gazing at the heavens and longing to speak with God, just as Moses, David, and other men of God did, of whom I had read in the Bible.

41. But no God would appear to me, and I had to return home sorrowful once more. Then I would seize the prayer books, open the windows, or sit under the open sky so that my reading might rise unhindered toward heaven. And thus I walked blindly for twenty-six years. <note>The author is describing a common struggle in 17th and 18th-century spirituality: the transition from "mechanical" or external religious practice to an internal, "heart-centered" experience.</note>

42. Therefore, I immediately laid down my New Testament and fell upon my knees again to thank God for this <term>manifestation of grace</term> <note>original: *Gnadenerscheinung*. In Pietist thought, this refers to a direct, felt experience of divine favor or a breakthrough in understanding.</note>; which flowed so abundantly that for five hours I could not reach an end, and I myself had to marvel at this rich blessing.

43. Immediately after, I also united my heart with Christ and surrendered myself entirely to Him in body, soul, and spirit, to willingly carry every cross <note>To "carry the cross" refers to accepting the suffering or social rejection that often followed a conversion to radical or dissenting religious views.</note> that might please Him, on the condition that He should not let me walk alone,

### Of the Reborn Person.
D 5

...since I was but an uncomprehending child who could not distinguish between left and right.

44. My dearest Helper <note>original: *mein liebster Gehülfe*. A Pietist term for Christ or the Holy Spirit as a constant companion.</note> also kindly and lovingly accepted this, and poured me a full cup that was indeed sweet in the mouth, but uncommonly bitter in the stomach. <note>This is an allusion to Revelation 10:10, symbolizing that while God's word is sweet, the life of a believer often involves "bitter" trials and suffering.</note>

45. But He never left me alone; rather, in all temptations, suffering, poverty, persecutions, and tribulations, He faithfully rescued me. To Him be eternal honor, praise, glory, and thanks!

46. I was also immediately enlightened with divine knowledge and driven in my spirit to rebuke the wicked life <note>Meaning he felt compelled to criticize the moral failings and "dead" faith of the established church and society.</note> and to present myself to the priests. They immediately rang the alarm bells and handed me over to the <term>authorities</term> <note>original: *Obrigkeit*. The local governing body or magistrate.</note> as a <term>heretic</term>, <term>enthusiast</term> <note>original: *Enthusiast*. In the 17th and 18th centuries, this was a derogatory label for someone who claimed direct, personal inspiration from the Holy Spirit.</note>, <term>visionary</term> <note>original: *Fantast*.</note>, and <term>Anabaptist</term> <note>original: *Wiedertäufer*. A radical religious group often persecuted by both Catholics and Lutherans for their beliefs on adult baptism and social equality.</note>, and so forth.

47. They subjected me to all kinds of mockery, scorn, and shame; they led me through the streets with <term>bailiffs</term> <note>original: *Büttel*. Minor court officials or guards.</note> and wanted to take my head. But because they could not all come to an agreement on the matter, they finally confiscated everything I owned and banished me from the city forever.

48. As I lay locked in such a stinking hole <note>A common contemporary term for a dark, unventilated prison cell or dungeon.</note>, the devil tried his first luck and tempted me with horrific despairs, urging me to seize the knife and release my anxious life from its torment, or to make a quick end of it with my neckcloth.

49. He also brought me into such fiery wrestling and struggle that the skin was worn away from my knees through kneeling, leaving them bare; yet because of the inner <term>anguish of soul</term> <note>original: *Seelenangst*. A profound spiritual and psychological distress common in "conversion narratives" of this period.</note>, I felt no outward pain. For the devil made all my prayers seem doubtful to me.

<summary>Following his conversion, the author is branded a radical heretic by the clergy and authorities, leading to his public shaming, banishment, and imprisonment. In the "stinking hole" of his cell, he describes a harrowing period of spiritual warfare and suicidal temptation.</summary>
<keywords>persecution, religious trial, banishment, spiritual warfare, soul-anguish, Pietism, radical Reformation</keywords>

### The Third Chapter.

50. As I was walking in the prison at noon in such anguish, and sang the Lutheran Psalm: *If God were not with us at this time* <note>original: "Wär’ GÖtt nicht mit uns dieſe Zeit." A famous hymn by Martin Luther based on Psalm 124, often sung by those facing persecution.</note> etc., I was suddenly carried away in the spirit and fell to the ground.

51. There I saw in the heart a bright white light; around the heart was a thick snake, wound three times into itself like a wreath. In the middle of the snake, in the bright light, Jesus appeared, in the form described by John in Revelation 1:13–15. <note>This passage describes Christ with hair like white wool, eyes like flame, and feet like burning bronze.</note>

52. He spoke with a very deep sigh: If your grace were not my comfort, O God, I would have to perish in my misery!

53. Hardly had these words been finished when the snake was crushed with a powerful motion and broken into so many pieces that I saw them fall down into the dark bowels as countless little fragments.

54. Thereupon I came back to myself and felt a noticeable relief and the power of faith in prayer; just as it has been depicted in this figure. <note>The author is referring to an illustration that would have appeared in the original printed book, showing the heart, the snake, and the light.</note>

55. This expulsion from the Egyptian house of bondage <note>original: *Aegyptisches Diensthaus*. A biblical metaphor referring to the soul's liberation from a life of sin, comparing it to the Israelites escaping slavery in Egypt.</note> is now the first beginning and entrance into the wilderness of temptation of unbelieving reason, which is not satisfied with the poverty of Christ; it is ashamed to beg, cannot dig <note>An allusion to the "unjust steward" in Luke 16:3, representing the soul's struggle to survive spiritually without its old comforts.</note>, and thinks of the fleshpots, cabbage, onions, and garlic of Egypt. And the devil first awakens unbelief, doubt, and mistrust.

56. Reason wants to see, and the faith in the heart is still but a tiny mustard seed; it cannot—

<summary>The author recounts a mystical vision in prison where Christ appears within his heart and crushes a coiled serpent, symbolizing a release from sin. This marks the beginning of a "wilderness" period where human reason struggles against new-found faith.</summary>

-># Of the Reborn Person.<-

56. ...not well against these storm-winds: there grumbling and murmuring begin, and the soul must sigh mightily.

57. However, what wonderful coincidences and rescues God sends, if the person only wishes to pray, I have experienced well through God’s grace; but it would be too much to recount here.

58. Therefore, I want to present two difficult temptations to the eager reader as a warning, in which I have seen many stumble and fall during my time.

59. The first temptation is: When the soul has been cast out of the Egyptian house of bondage <note>original: *Aegyptisches Diensthaus*. A common biblical metaphor for the state of sin and spiritual slavery, comparing the soul's journey to the Israelites escaping Egypt.</note> by the spirit of this world and placed into the poverty of Christ, so that she <note>In the original German, "soul" (*Seele*) is a feminine noun, and the author personifies her as a bride seeking her groom.</note> should work in the vineyard of God, place her earthly hunger in God’s promises, and also—with her faithful helper and Bridegroom of the Soul, Jesus—through her desire for faith, prayer, and supplication, build, form, and create her necessities in the inner heaven, so that such things might be substantially cast to her on earth and sent by charitable, pious hearts:

60. If she turns back toward Egypt into earthly reason, hungering for earthly bread, and accepts, seizes, and grasps whatever comes her way <term>accidentally <note>original: *casualiter*. Meaning things that happen by chance or through worldly circumstances rather than divine intent.</note></term> as a divine providence, rather than taking it for a diabolical temptation; and if she does not pray diligently, does not stand in constant fear of the tempter, and does not keep sharp watch over Christ’s footsteps within the mind <note>original: *Gemüth*. A central term in German mysticism referring to the deep seat of emotions, will, and thoughts.</note>.

61. Through this, many a brave soul has been deceived, who afterwards lamented and sighed:

### The Third Chapter.

Oh, if only I had not trusted! Oh, if only I had held fast! Yet it cannot be changed afterward.

62. For then the Heavenly Virgin <note>original: *himmlische Jungfrau*. In Böhme’s mysticism, this represents Divine Wisdom or Sophia, the spiritual counterpart and guide to the human soul.</note> shuts herself within her center <note>original: *Centrum*. The inner point of divine origin or the heart of the spiritual self.</note>, and leaves the soul outside to knock and wait in vain.

63. And even though reason <note>original: *Vernunft*. Here referring to human intellectual understanding as opposed to divine revelation.</note> has received a glimpse of the divine light, so that it sharply understands God's mysteries and the mouth can chatter about them; yet the shaping, forming, and creative power is gone, and only a sounding barrel <note>original: *tönend Faß*. Likely an allusion to the "sounding brass" or "clanging cymbal" of 1 Corinthians 13; meaning hollow talk without spiritual substance.</note> remains.

64. The devil also hungers for God’s love; but see to it that he does not swallow it up for you and take possession of your house, which has been swept clean with brooms <note>A reference to Matthew 12:44, where an evil spirit returns to find a soul "swept and put in order," only to bring more demons in.</note>, for which he has a great desire.

65. And where he enters again, he takes seven spirits with him who are worse than himself, and locks your soul and mind <note>original: *Gemüth*. The seat of emotions, will, and spiritual disposition.</note> within the <term>seven forms of nature <note>original: *sieben Gestalten der Natur*. A central concept in this theology describing the seven properties or qualities through which God reveals Himself in the world.</note></term>, so that you will not easily get him out again afterward. Therefore, hold fast to what you have; suffering need is a bad guest.

66. The second temptation is even heavier and more ruinous: When the soul, ignited with God's fire, burns brightly and brings forth a beautiful light, in which the Heavenly Virgin rises high and triumphing in the mind, and kisses her dear Bridegroom with her sweet rays of light in the fire-soul <note>original: *Feuerseele*. The soul in its energetic, desiring, and "burning" state before it is tempered by the light of Love.</note>, and gives her the powers to reveal and bring forth God’s wonders, so that she—through her strong desire in prayer—forms and even makes substantial God’s powers and wonders, and appears in heaven with a heartfelt song of praise:

<summary>This page warns the soul against losing its spiritual grounding after initial enlightenment, describing how the "Heavenly Virgin" (Wisdom) withdraws if the soul yields to the devil's hunger. It explains the danger of spiritual pride where one understands mysteries intellectually but lacks the actual creative power of God.</summary>

# Of the Reborn Person.

67. If the soul departs from humility and yieldedness <note>original: *Gelassenheit*. A central concept in German mysticism, referring to a state of complete surrender to God and detachment from the self.</note> and enters into self-love and self-gratification, thinking that its power is its own soul-fire-power, and that it can perform and give birth to such wonders through its own fiery magic and prayer; it then flies high above the thrones, wanting to be something, and thereby makes itself into a proud, self-willed devil. Under the appearance of divine justice, it wants to bow everything under its feet and ride over it; and whatever does not bow quickly and instantly, it pursues with fire and sword, and curses and damns to hell.

68. At this, the dear Virgin <note>referring to Sophia, the personification of Divine Wisdom</note> becomes very distressed and troubled, and can come to the aid of her bridegroom <note>the soul</note> with no love.

69. For even when she wants to introduce her sweet rays of light into the soul's fire to soften it, the soul only becomes fiercer, more proud, and more aspiring; it resists the gentle light with all its might and lets nothing in that would break or take away its sharpness.

70. It regards all gentleness as hypocrisy and rejects everything that is not fire; this is covered up with the <term>mystery of folly <note>original: *Mysterio stultitiæ*. This refers to a spiritual blindness where one mistakes their own ego and anger for religious zeal or divine "mystery."</note></term> and called **God's** justice and judgment; and yet under such a cover, it is only a proud, high-flying devil.

71. Now, when the dear Virgin Sophia sees that she cannot save her dear bridegroom with any love or gentleness, she withdraws into her light-principle <note>original: *Lichtsprincipium*. The essential realm or source of divine light.</note>, making the soul-fire

### The Third Chapter.

[is] dark, and lets it fall into sin and folly; He gives him a fickle, foolish wife <note>In the mystical tradition of writers like Jacob Böhme, a "foolish wife" or "earthly water" refers to worldly burdens or domestic cares that God uses to "tether" a prideful soul, preventing it from flying too high into dangerous spiritual arrogance.</note> at his neck, so that the great firebrand may be quenched with earthly water, and so the fiery spirit in his imagination has something to play with, and through such a bond, his flying might be prevented.

72. 73. But the dear **God** looked upon my simplicity with mercy and released me in time; for which I remain eternally grateful to Him.

74. Therefore, let the good-hearted reader be faithfully warned: for it is much better to become wise through another’s harm than through one's own.

75. Indeed, nothing great or small can be born without fire, whether in external or internal nature. It is also true that where there is a great fire, there is likewise a great light; which is very good and useful, provided it burns in humility and does not step out of its order to devour, ignite, and consume everything.

76. Had Lucifer in heaven and Adam in paradise taken note of this, the former would have remained an angel and the latter a paradisiacal man.

# The Fourth Chapter.

## Preface.

->I.<-

1. Here follow, in order, the author's third and fourth figures, which have been placed at the very beginning and as the title of all the writer’s works, because they represent the first image of **GOD** from the <note>original: *Wiedergeburt*</note> rebirth in Christ in its perfection, with the <term>Pectorale</term> <note>The priestly breastplate (Latin: *pectorale*) worn by the High Priest in the Old Testament, containing twelve stones.</note> or priestly breast-shield of both eternal <term>Principles</term> <note>original: *Principien*. In this mystical system, these are the fundamental realms of reality: the dark fire-world and the holy light-world.</note> in <term>Urim</term> and <term>Thummim</term> <note>Hebrew terms usually translated as "Lights and Perfections," referring to the sacred objects used by the High Priest to determine God's will.</note>, the light and perfection of the spirit, as well as with the <term>Humerale</term> <note>A liturgical shoulder-garment or cape (Latin: *humerale*), signifying the "government upon his shoulder."</note> and royal shoulder-mantle of the kingdom of **JESUS** over the outer <term>Principle</term> <note>The physical, visible world.</note>, as can be read in Isaiah 9:6.

2. This is so that a reader may notice and see right at the start where the author is aiming in all these letters; like Paul, he labors to the utmost to represent every person perfectly in that same image in **JESUS**.

3. This is the goal, in which the <term>Centers</term> <note>original: *Centra*. The focal points of spiritual energy or "wheels" within the human soul.</note> in the new man stand in their original divine order; the first and second <term>Principles</term> at the front, and the third <term>Principle</term> at the back of the mind.

4. All three <term>Original States</term> <note>original: *Urständen*. The primordial foundations or fundamental conditions of being.</note> are in the heart and reveal themselves within it through the baptism of the Holy Spirit, in such a way that the fire-world opens up below, and the light-world opens up above.

5. The air-world on the back is an outgrowth of the first two, which is why in its mirror

## Of the Inner Man, according to the Image of God and the Divine Dwelling in Him, as in His Temple.

->I.<-

1. This figure depicts the threefold inner **angelic** man <note>original: *englischen*. In this mystical context, it refers to the state of humanity as originally intended by God—pure and radiant like an angel.</note>, as he was created by God, and is renewed and glorified again in the **rebirth**.

2. However, it has cost me such a **fiery earnestness** before it was born into the light of day, that I cannot express it; you will likely experience it as well, dear reader, if you come to these rows.

3. The outer sphere or world is the **anxious mind**, with the seven forms of nature <note>A reference to the "Seven Properties of Nature" in the theology of Jacob Boehme, which describe the process by which spiritual essence manifests into material reality.</note>, which contains within itself the sensory governance along with the understanding, both spiritual and physical.

4. And it is a mirror of the inner dark **fire-world** and **light-world**, and is bound to both throughout the entire time of the outer earthly life.

5. Therefore, it is also stuck in a most wretched **squeeze** <note>original: *Quetsche*. A mystical term for the soul being compressed or pressured between the opposing forces of heaven and hell.</note>, as can be seen from the accompanying figure, which I have placed on its back for that purpose.

6. Now the devil looks at it from his dark hell from below, out of the dark fire-root; now he looks from above or outside from the stars, so that it stands in constant conflict and adversity, lying now above, now below;

7. And it always longs for the gentle **light-ground** of eternal love, with constant desire and—

16. “As David then prays in his Psalms,
‘throw my enemies behind my back’; likewise, ‘tread
my enemies under my feet.’ To the experienced,
practiced warriors, this is well understood.” <note>These references to the Psalms (such as Psalm 110:1) are interpreted here not as physical warfare, but as the internal spiritual struggle to subdue one's own lower impulses or "enemies" within the soul.</note>

17. In the heart is the divine **Light-world** or
**Eye**, as the temple of the Holy Spirit, wherein
God dwells, and is named God alone in the Light;
and it is the middle principle <note>original: *Principium*. In this mystical system, reality is divided into three "principles": the dark fire-world, the light-world, and the outer physical world. The heart is the bridge.</note> in the
reborn new man.

18. And down beneath it is the divine magical
**Eye of Wonder and Fire**, (*see Jacob Boehme’s On the Incarnation <note>original: "vide J. Boehms Incarnat." referring to *De Incarnatione Verbi*.</note>, Part I, Chapter 3, verse 19*) which in those
who are reborn is the place of God’s love,
from which the Father gives birth to his Son. In us
who are reborn, it is God’s wrath-fire. <note>This paradoxical statement suggests that the "fire" is the fundamental energy of God; in the holy, it manifests as the warmth of love, but in its raw, foundational state—even within the seeker—it is the consuming "fire of wrath" that must be transformed.</note>

19. For it is the foundation of Heaven and of
Hell, and of the visible world, from which good
and evil originate, as Light and Darkness, Life
and Death, Salvation and Damnation:

20. And it is not unjustly called the ***Mysterium Magnum*** <note>original: "Great Mystery." A central concept in Boehmian philosophy referring to the primordial "chaos" or "storehouse" of all possibilities from which the universe was created.</note>, wherein two beings and wills
are understood, namely the Unity <note>original: *Einheit*. The state of being one with God.</note> and the differentiated
will <note>original: *schiedliche Wille*. The individual will that separates itself from the whole to pursue its own desires.</note>, which introduces itself into a desire,
until it emerges from Fire and Light; where in the Fire, the natural
life is understood, and in the Light, the holy spiritual life of
Unity is understood.

21. Thus it also gives birth out of itself to two wills:
a fiery, flying, proud **devil’s-will**,
and a humble, sinking **angelic-will**,
from which the choice in the "harvesting into the One" arises. <note>original: *Einernte*. A specialized term likely referring to the "gathering" or "ingathering" of the soul's fragmented desires back into the divine Unity.</note>

22. For man is, in this time, his own
maker; he can lead his desire into himself,
as into a selfhood, or into God’s Unity in

...placed into serenity <note>original: *Gelassenheit*. A central term in German mysticism meaning "yieldedness" or "letting go" of the self-will to allow God to act.</note>: where he is then taken in by either wrath or love.

23. For whatever the mind invites immediately burns within the mind, whether it be an earthly or heavenly essence; and it gives birth from itself to such a spirit in words and works.

24. If the will now burns in love, it is pure Paradise; but where it breaks away from love, it is a pure Hell.

25. This is the great **fire-soul**, according to the Father's eternal fire-nature; and in the heart is the eternal **light-fire-nature**, according to the Son's property; although they are not separated from one another. <note>In this theology, the Father represents the "fire" or raw power of existence, and the Son represents the "light" or love that softens and directs that power.</note>

26. According to the third principle <note>original: *Principio*. The third principle refers to the physical, outer world created from the interaction of light and darkness.</note> it is also the **air-soul**, known as the spirit of the world <note>original: *Spiritus Mundi*.</note>, and it burns in both heat and cold.

27. Here it should also be noted that this is not a material fire, but a spiritual one, whose center or root is the eternal darkness—the reason why God calls Himself a "consuming fire." <note>A reference to Hebrews 12:29 and Deuteronomy 4:24.</note>

28. "The magical fire-eye on the left side beneath the heart is, in its root, God's wrath-fire, according to which God calls Himself an 'angry and jealous God'; and according to the light, it is God's love-fire, after which He alone is named God."

29. "According to the root, Jacob Boehme calls this spirit the 'spirit of the great world,' which mocked Adam after his fall (Genesis 3:22). It is also the Cherub with the fiery sword; also the destroying angel <note>original: *Würgengel*.</note> in Egypt; and also the angel who gave the laws to the children of Israel on Mount Sinai."

<meta>catchword: 30.</meta>

# The Fourth Chapter.

...Paradise and the dwelling of the heavenly Sophia <note>Sophia is the personification of Divine Wisdom in Christian mystical tradition, often seen as the "bride" of the purified soul.</note> became manifest, and was brought forth.

37. What kind of lovely taste, smell, hearing, seeing, and feeling this gave in the mind and the whole body, I cannot describe.

38. The reader must only earnestly press into the rebirth <note>The process of spiritual transformation where the soul is "born again" from the worldly state into a divine one.</note> and come to the **Wedding of the Lamb**, then he will experience what occurs in the heavenly marriage, and thereafter he will have to stammer himself, being unable to express anything of it.

39. The two circles turned back-to-back are the two eternal principles <note>original: *Principia*. These represent the fundamental dualities of existence in Boehme’s thought.</note>, or eyes of the soul: Love and Wrath, Light and Darkness; about which [Jacob] Boehme is to be read in the first "Question of the Soul." <note>A reference to Boehme's work *Forty Questions on the Soul* (1620).</note>

40. Into whichever principle the soul now turns itself, therein it sees, and is a stranger to the other; for it stands back-to-back against the same.

41. And no man can see further than into his mother <note>In this context, "mother" refers to the source or fundamental principle—either the Light world or the Dark world—from which a person's current consciousness is born.</note>, and speak of it; each principle carries its own understanding, as is to be seen in Acts 12:12, 13.

42. Now, if the reader reflects upon the figure of the first chapter, he shall easily be able to come to an understanding of how God reveals Himself in us humans according to time and eternity, and how we are formed as His true divine image and likeness according to and out of all three worlds:

43. It also depends only on this alone: that we, with our soul, turn away from the starry reason <note>original: *gestirnten Vernunft*. This refers to human intellect that is limited to the physical, "astral" world and the influence of the cosmos, rather than being illuminated by the divine.</note> and place our will and desire into the innermost **ground of light**. <note>original: *Lichtsgrund*. The deepest, most central point of the soul where God's light dwells.</note>

It instructs the reader to turn away from worldly, astral intellect toward the internal divine light to understand their true nature as an image of God.</summary>

### Concerning the Inner Man, etc.

44. Since the Holy Spirit then immediately meets us in our heart, kisses and embraces our will and desire, and represents us again before our dear heavenly Father in the hidden majestic light through **Jesus**, just as **Joseph** presented his father and five youngest brothers before **Pharaoh**. <note>A reference to Genesis 47, where Joseph brings his family into the presence of the Egyptian ruler; here used as an allegory for Christ presenting the redeemed soul to God the Father.</note>

45. Who is immediately and heartily rejoiced that his lost son has come back to Him in humility, and kisses the outer nature with the five senses very kindly and perceptibly, as all this is sufficiently known to reborn children. <note>In this mystical tradition, "reborn children" are those who have undergone a spiritual transformation or "new birth."</note>

46. Now it shall also be shown to the reader from whence the Fall in such a beautiful image took its origin; that it was by no means the will of **God**, as human reason teaches <note>The author distinguishes between "reason" (worldly logic) and spiritual revelation.</note>; but rather **Adam’s** own fault, who was created very good by **God** and received the free will to impregnate and propagate himself.

47. For he had both **Tinctures** <note>In theosophy, "Tinctures" refer to the two fundamental spiritual qualities—often the "fire" (masculine/soul) and "light" (feminine/spirit)—which were perfectly balanced in the original human.</note> in him, and was a male virgin, <note>original: *männliche Jungfrau*. This refers to the belief that the original Adam was an androgynous being, possessing both masculine and feminine spiritual qualities before the creation of Eve.</note> clothed with wisdom and understanding, so that he could rule over fish, birds, and all animals, and could also give names to each according to its property, as **Moses** writes in **Genesis** 1 and 2, and presents the Fall with very clear words.

48. Namely, that **God** spoke: It would not be good for **Adam** to be alone; He would make him a helper.

49. So the question is: How that which **God** created good could have become evil; since the

# The Fourth Chapter

...the tree of temptation, alongside the prohibition, had not yet been manifest?

50. Thus we must indeed say, by virtue of Christ's revelation in Luke 15:12, *and he divided his living among them* <note>original Greek: "καὶ διεῖλεν αὐτοῖς τὸν βίον." This verse from the Parable of the Prodigal Son is used here as an allegory for the division of the unified human nature.</note>, that **Adam** lusted to have the forms of life divided and separated into a woman, so that he might be like all the animals.

51. Since this also occurred immediately, but was against **God’s** first order and image; thus the Fall is not first to be sought in the bite of the apple.

52. For although **Adam** did not eat from the forbidden fruit with his outer mouth, his **imagination** <note>In this mystical tradition, "imagination" is not just fantasy, but a creative, formative power of the soul that can "impregnate" the spirit with desires.</note> entered so strongly into the forbidden tree that he was overcome by it, and died to the inner **life of power** <note>The spiritual, energetic life that sustained his original, immortal form.</note>, or, as the Scripture says, fell into sleep.

53. Now we wish to investigate further: from whence did the desire for the dividing of the forms of life arise in **Adam**? Thus we must say that it grew within him from the **principles** <note>The fundamental spiritual forces or "worlds" (fire, light, and the physical world) that constitute the human being.</note> or forms of life themselves, and by no means from **God**, who is eternal love and good, and can will no evil.

54. I have already mentioned in the previous chapter that fire is inevitable and good, as long as it shines, warms, and serves man:

55. So likewise **God's** fire of wrath is necessary, useful, and good, when it burns in our soul in love and humility as it does in **God**, and does not step out of its order.

It emphasizes that the Fall was rooted in Adam's own "imagination" and the internal "fire of wrath" rather than a direct action from God.</summary>

->**Of the Inward Man, etc.**<-

56. Now, the **fire of wrath** <note>In this mystical tradition, "wrath-fire" is the fundamental, harsh energy of existence—associated with God the Father—which must be softened and transformed by the "love-fire" or light of the Son.</note> is indeed a begetter of the **fire of love** or light, and a father of the Son: but when the wrath wishes to rule over the light, and to cast it under itself or swallow it up, then strife and disorder arise in the essences of the light, and it must be extinguished.

57. So that thereafter the fire also no longer has any essence <note>Meaning the spiritual "fuel" or substance that allows the light to shine.</note> for its burning and becomes a darkness, as can be seen in sulfur; as it also happened to the devil, as seen in Genesis 1:2, where it says that it was entirely dark upon the deep.

58. This **fire of wrath**, as the **dark principle**, was now in **Adam** the first mover toward desire, for it stood hidden within him in the light; yet it also wished to be manifest and active in its own power.

59. The second mover was the **spirit of the world** <note>original Latin: *Spiritus Mundi*. This refers to the planetary and elemental influences of the physical universe.</note>, or the **airy soul**, which stands with its root in the dark principle, and which was meant to nourish and raise **Adam**; yet it was, as it were, swallowed up by both inner lives and had to be subject to them.

60. This too wished to be manifest, and to lead its own governance according to the outer senses—to perceive, taste, and know every quality in its own characteristic.

61. But the middle one—the **principle of light**—stood in the way and held dominion over the other two.

62. This conflict and hunger for the supreme governance was now in **Adam’s** soul, but without compulsion; **Adam** was able, through the power of the **principle of light**,

### The Fourth Chapter.

...[dominion and] could well restrain <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page, referring to the power of the "light principle" to keep the harsher forces in check.</note> those forces wherein God alone is called God.

In this context, it refers to the physical universe and its elemental influences.</note>, until he was finally impregnated and overcome by it; he fell into a faint, as if into a sleep, and was divided.

64. Whoever rightly understands and grasps this foundation of the **principles** <term>Principles: The three fundamental forces of existence according to Böhme: 1. Dark fire/will, 2. Divine light/love, and 3. The physical world.</term>, to him all the hidden secrets of the Scripture are quite clearly perceptible.

65. Just as from this division of the principles, three kinds of humans grew out thereafter; as can be seen in the three sons of **Noah**, by whom the world is populated, and has spread itself into three kinds of life-forms.

66. And although there are many races and ways of life among the people in the world, including a mixed way of living, they nevertheless all stand under these three principles—that is, one principle holds the chief dominion over the others in a given person.

67. For all three principles are in every human, but not all three are active, weaving, and perceptible.

68. As Adam did then, so we all do today, alas, even the **reborn** <note>Those who have undergone a spiritual awakening or conversion.</note> are in part not excluded; we play the hypocrite to all three principles.

69. We seek the outer kingdom, strive for money, property, honor, good days, and easy living; we serve the spirit of this world, go to church and to the **Lord’s Supper**, and worship the **spirit of the world** <note>original Latin: *Spiritum Mundi*</note> in blindness out of prayer books, so that he [might]...

72. In the author's theology, this "self" must spiritually "die" to be reborn in the divine light.</term>, which death will surely make certain at its end.

<meta>This page continues the critique of religious hypocrisy. The previous page ended with the beginning of a thought about how people pray for God to bless their worldly interests; this page completes that sentence and explores the "cloak of piety" used to hide selfish motives.</meta>

### Concerning the Inner Man, etc.

...bless and want to preserve our unjust and transitory **Mammon** <term>Mammon: A biblical term for wealth or material possessions, often personified as a false god or a corrupting influence.</term>.

70. We serve the Devil under a glistening and hypocritical cloak of prudence, wisdom, and piety, so that we do not speak the truth from the heart, nor dare to call darkness darkness, lest it happen to us as the proverb says: *He who lashes out with the truth will have the fiddle-bow struck against his head* <note>A German idiom (Wer die Wahrheit geisget...) meaning that those who speak harsh truths often face immediate, painful consequences or social backlash.</note>.

71. And when we are often forced to step into the light to save our honor and good reputation, we act so wisely that no one might "get at our skin" <note>An idiom meaning to be criticized or physically harmed.</note>; or it is said that we should not judge or condemn others so that we are not judged in return; which indeed has an appearance of piety.

72. In this way, the Devil remains covered up, and we remain "fine, pious people" who want to live softly without the **Cross** <note>In this context, the "Cross" represents the necessary suffering, self-denial, and external persecution required to live a truly spiritual life.</note>, be godly without persecution, and enter the Kingdom of God without tribulation.

73. We act the hypocrite toward dear God for the sake of the Kingdom of Heaven; for we would also gladly be saved. We give Him many good words, speak of the **Following of Christ** <note>Also known as the "Imitation of Christ," referring to the spiritual practice of living according to Jesus's teachings and suffering.</note>, join in fellowship with upright Christians, and even do good deeds so that people will praise us.

74. And we think that God will take us into Heaven out of grace without the **dying-off of selfhood** <term>Selbheit: "Selfhood" or the ego. In mystical tradition, this refers to the selfish will that must "die" or be surrendered before a soul can be united with the Divine.</term>, which death will surely make certain at its end.

75. But listen: God does not ask for "mouth-following" <note>German: *Mundnachfolgen*; meaning empty words or a superficial profession of faith without a change of heart.</note> or babbling; rather, He wants the body,

Referring to the "Woman clothed with the sun" who has the moon under her feet; in this mystical context, it signifies a soul that has risen above the changing, "moon-like" nature of the material world.</note>, and have trusted God for even a piece of bread.

80. For He Himself takes care of His poor Christians, gives His most holy faith into their hearts, and does not let them go begging.

# On the Inner Man, etc.

...will love him, and manifest myself to him, teaches Christ in John 14:21 <note>original: "Joh. 14, 21."</note>.

82. Where no love for **Jesus** burns in the heart, there is also no joy or desire for **discipleship** <term>Nachfolge: The act of following Christ's life and suffering as a practical model for one's own life, a central concept in German Pietism.</term>, nor any power in the will to ask God for His Holy Spirit and its governance, because He leads us precisely out of the world and out of ourselves into the life of Jesus Christ.

83. At which our nature is terrified, as we see in Christ Himself, when he wrestled with God’s wrath in our humanity on the **Mount of Olives** <note>original: "Oelberg." Referring to the Agony in the Garden of Gethsemane, where Christ’s human nature recoiled from the impending suffering.</note>. And from this it comes to pass that so few are saved, because they love their earthly life more than Christ's life.

84. Just as the love for the earthly makes a person strong, so that he regards no effort, labor, danger, sorrow, nor worry, only to attain the temporal and **unrighteous mammon** <term>Mammon: A biblical term for wealth or material possessions, often personified as a false god that enslaves the heart.</term>, honor, splendor, and **pleasure** <term>Wohllust: Sensual or worldly pleasure; in this context, the indulgence of the physical senses at the expense of the spirit.</term>;

85. So also the love for **Jesus** makes a Christian strong to take up all suffering, the **cross** <term>Creutz: Both the literal instrument of Christ's death and the metaphorical hardships a believer must endure.</term>, poverty, persecution, and **tribulation** <term>Trübsal: Deep distress or affliction; the "narrow way" of the Christian life.</term> and to willingly carry it after Him.

86. For love makes Christ's **yoke** <term>Joch: A wooden beam used between a pair of oxen to pull a load; used here to represent the submission of the will to Christ’s discipline.</term> and burden soft and light; love drives out all fear, anxiety, and trepidation, and constantly awakens a new desire for more suffering.

87. For the person always feels comfort, joy, and strength in the heart, and experiences that God never forsakes; but tears and rescues one out of hell, death, and distress, also giving one victory after another.

88. Even if someone already had faith and could move mountains, but did not have this love within them,

# Of the Inward Man, etc.

...I will love him, and manifest myself to him, teaches **Christ** in **John 14:21** <note>original: "Joh. 14, 21."</note>.

82. Where no love for **Jesus** burns in the heart, there is neither desire nor longing for the **imitation** <term>Nachfolge: A central concept in German mysticism and Pietism, referring to the "Imitation of Christ"—actively modeling one's life, especially through suffering and humility, after the life of Jesus.</term> [of Him], nor any power in the will to ask **God** for His **Holy Spirit** and His guidance; for He leads us precisely out of the world and out of ourselves into the life of **Jesus Christ**.

83. Our nature recoils at this, as we see in **Christ** Himself when He wrestled with the wrath of **God** in His humanity on the **Mount of Olives** <note>original: "Oelberg." Referring to the Agony in the Garden of Gethsemane where Jesus prayed before His arrest.</note>. And this is also why so few are saved: because they love their earthly life more than the life of **Christ**.

84. Just as love for earthly things makes a person strong, so that he disregards effort, labor, danger, grief, and worry, only to attain temporal and unrighteous **mammon** <term>Mammon: A biblical term for wealth or material property, often portrayed as a corrupting influence that competes with God for a person's devotion.</term>, honor, splendor, and pleasure;

85. So too does the love for **Jesus** make a Christian strong to take up all suffering, the **cross**, poverty, persecution, and tribulation, and to willingly carry them after **Him**.

86. For love makes **Christ's** yoke and burden soft and light; love drives out all fear, anxiety, and trembling, and continually awakens a new desire for more suffering.

87. For a person always feels comfort, joy, and strength in the heart, and experiences that **God** never forsakes; rather, He tears and rescues one from hell, death, and distress, and grants one victory after another.

88. Even if one already had **faith** and could move mountains, but did not have this love within him—

### The Fourth Chapter.

88. ...without it, he would be but a sounding bell. <note>original: "klingende Schelle." A reference to 1 Corinthians 13:1; in modern Bibles often translated as "clanging cymbal."</note> This love is mightier than death, which no floods of water can drown.

89. And when you receive this love into your heart, and are thereby set aflame in your soul, take great care that you do not let it be extinguished or swallowed up again, for the devil hungers for it as well.

90. Therefore, I will now show you the foundation of how it happens in the **rebirth** <term>Wiedergeburt: A central Pietist and mystical concept referring to the spiritual "New Birth" or regeneration of a person through divine grace.</term>, just as I myself have experienced: When the soul turns inward like the **Prodigal Son** and begins to remember its Father’s house again, it has no strength or ability of its own to proceed; **Adam** has squandered it. <note>original: "verbuhlet." This term suggests the soul was "wasted" or lost through illicit love/attachment to the world rather than God.</note>

91. But **Christ** immediately comes to the aid of the heart with His Spirit and ignites the **vital forms** <term>Lebensgestalten: The inward spiritual qualities or "shapes" that constitute the soul’s life and character.</term> of the soul, so that the **Light-principle** <term>Lichtsprincipium: In theosophical mysticism, this is the divine spark or the realm of God's light and love that remains dormant in the soul until awakened.</term>, which until now had been motionless and hidden within it, becomes active and perceptible again.

92. Through this, the soul receives strength to set out on the way and to draw near to **God** in prayer; yet it still stands in the dark body and cannot yet recognize its own love and its own will, nor can it thoroughly humble itself or surrender to **God’s** most dear will:

93. Thus **Christ** ignites with His light-fire the **magical fire-eye** <term>magische Feuerauge: A term from Jacob Böhme’s mysticism referring to the root of the human will or the soul's "vision," which can either burn with the fire of anger or the light of love.</term> (which the devil murdered in **Adam**, thereby stirring up the wrath), so that it burns brightly in love and shines as light in the heart; then the soul is radiated through and set aglow.

94. Then, for the first time, she sees her hideous form, and how she is bound to a hundred animalistic qualities. Oh, how she laments! How she cries and weeps! She would gladly crawl through a mouse hole, even if her skin and hair were left behind. <note>The "mouse hole" is a folk metaphor expressing extreme shame and the desire to hide or escape a state of worthlessness.</note>

95. Indeed, she would undress herself, if it were in her power, so that she might only be clothed over with her heavenly **Sophia**. <term>Sophia: In Christian mysticism, Sophia is the personification of Divine Wisdom and the spiritual bride of the soul.</term>

96. Oh! what vows she makes, what loyalty she pledges! Indeed, she resolves to **spiritually circumcise** herself for the sake of her dear bride, and to renounce all earthly things, to willingly take up every cross, to follow through death and distress, and to remain faithful with body, soul, and spirit as His very own until the end. <term>spiritually circumcise: A metaphor for cutting away worldly attachments and the desires of the "flesh" to prepare the soul for God.</term>

97. The dear **Sophia** hears this from her **beloved** with joy and kisses him inwardly; she draws her essence into the soul and covers all sin. <note>original: "Buhlen." In modern German, this can mean a paramour or lover in a negative sense, but in mystical writing, it refers to the soul as the intimate, cherished partner of Divine Wisdom.</note>

98. Now, when the soul has walked for a time with her dear bride in the **rose garden**, and has gathered all sorts of beautiful little flowers and **powerful sayings** into her heart, the dear bride then gathers her dear beloved—the soul—together out of the whole body. <note>rose garden: A common mystical setting representing a state of spiritual peace and communion with the Divine.</note> <term>Kraftsprüche: Short, potent scriptural verses or prayers believed to provide spiritual strength.</term>

99. Then the soul looks like a **fiery sphere** (see the third figure, and observe the majestic **Wonder-eye** of **Jacob Böhme**), and she plunges it into the fiery sea (as also happened to me five times in succession over five days, while I was at prayer in the evening; there I saw that in the center it was crystalline-blue, like the clear sky, but it was a fiery water, which over... <note>Wonder-eye: A central concept in the theosophy of Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), symbolizing the "Eye of Eternity" or the divine perspective from which all things originate.</note>

# Chapter Four.

100. And [He] baptizes the soul with his holy magical fire, and also with his Holy Spirit in the heart—as already reported above in section 36—and leads her into the **wilderness of the flesh**, so that she may be tempted by the devil in the **spirit of the world**. <note>original: "Spiritu Mundi." In theosophy, this refers to the "world spirit" or the astral influence that governs physical existence and mundane desires.</note>

101. Then the true trial begins: For the Bride <note>Sophia, or Divine Wisdom, who acts as the soul's spiritual guide and partner.</note> withdraws into her own **principle**, and so the devil comes in the **form of an angel** and says to her: "Why are you so troubled? Make bread out of stones; you have great faith, and you do not even know it yourself." <term>principle: Based on Jacob Boehme’s philosophy, a "principle" (principium) is one of the three fundamental realms of reality: the dark fire-world, the light world, or the material world.</term>

102. When the soul then walks in **humility**, placing her hunger in the **Word of the Lord**, he retreats and puts on another **mask**. He attributes great powers to the soul, suggesting that she is something special and called by God to perform great miracles in the world. <note>original: "Verbum Domini" (Word of the Lord). The author warns that the devil tries to replace true faith with spiritual pride.</note>

103. If the soul then surrenders herself to God in **humility**, accepting whatever He may wish to work with and through her, he [the devil] must also withdraw.

104. But he comes in the **form of a cunning serpent**, and presents to the soul—within the **third principle**—wealth and financial gain, honor and outward status, and even wealthy marriages. <term>third principle: The visible, material world in which we live, often seen as a place of distraction and temporary illusion.</term>

105. These appear exactly as if they were sent from heaven by God Himself, without any desire or seeking from the person, so that the soul often stands there in great wonder, and many are entangled and trapped within them...

106. 107. 108. In this mystical tradition, "imagination" is the creative faculty of the soul; if directed toward worldly things, it "impregnates" the soul with earthly desires.</note> presented by the tempter, she is immediately impregnated and ensnared by earthly lust, which awakens desire and drives the will toward the deed; so that often, out of the deepest love, the greatest bitterness and wrath can arise, and dear brothers can become bitter enemies and persecutors.

109. These then cast into the lowest hell that which they previously praised as high as heaven with hand and mouth, and had testified to with sensible truth; they then cry it out as false, fantasy, and mere opinion—as I have, alas, experienced with great sorrow.

110. Therefore, a beginning **student**, when he has received a kiss from his dear **Sophia** in his soul, must diligently guard himself against **flying** <note>A metaphor for spiritual pride or "soaring" into high mysteries before one is spiritually grounded.</note> and must by no means think that he is ready immediately for the wedding...
